````math
## Appendix contents

## Appendix A Related work

Harness evolution. Recent methods improve an agent’s harness with an LLM
proposer that reads execution traces and scores; our four baselines
differ in how the proposer reads this evidence ([Lee et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib21); [Lin et al., 2026](#bib.bib23); [Zhang et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib55); [Chen et al., 2026c](#bib.bib9))
(Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Baseline reimplementation details ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")).
Related work adapts harnesses at test time ([Nie et al.,
2026](#bib.bib29)), refines prompt-level harnesses from pairwise
comparisons ([Lee et al., 2026a](#bib.bib20)), optimizes prompts and
programs with LLM feedback ([Shinn et al., 2023](#bib.bib35); [Yang
et al., 2024](#bib.bib48); [Khattab et al., 2024](#bib.bib19)), updates
model weights ([Hebbar et al., 2026](#bib.bib13)), or distills skills
from trajectories ([Ni et al., 2026](#bib.bib28); [Alzubi et al.,
2026](#bib.bib2)). [Wang et al. (2026b)](#bib.bib42) note that many
results search and report on the same tasks; ModularRSI evolves on tasks
disjoint from its benchmarks ([Wu et al., 2026](#bib.bib45)), and RRSI
regularizes proposal and selection and reports out-of-distribution
gains ([Xia et al., 2026](#bib.bib46)).

Verifying harness edits. Execution is also used to screen candidate
harnesses with gates ([Luo et al., 2026b](#bib.bib26)), repair
attributed failures ([Chen et al., 2026b](#bib.bib8)), and revert and
replay agent runs ([Yu et al., 2026](#bib.bib51)). SPEAR gives a prompt
optimizer an evaluate tool that it calls itself ([Lu et al.,
2026](#bib.bib24)), and VeRO finds that agent optimizers mostly edit
prompts ([Ursekar et al., 2026](#bib.bib40)). Among contemporaneous
systems, AutoSaddler keeps a patch only if it improves a mini-batch,
then evaluates it on a development set ([Park et al.,
2026](#bib.bib31)), HarnessEvolve gates updates and locates errors
against reference trajectories ([Jiang et al., 2026](#bib.bib16)), and
EnvHarness accepts environment components by success rates over fresh
rollouts ([Huang et al., 2026](#bib.bib15)).

Evolving the optimizer. In most harness optimizers, the optimizer’s own
procedure stays fixed during a run; when the optimizer and the executor
share a base model, the optimizer runs under its own prompts or
scaffold ([Lin et al., 2026](#bib.bib23); [Nie et al.,
2026](#bib.bib29)). In Self-Harness and HASE, the model proposes edits
under the harness it is editing, so one harness serves both
roles ([Zhang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib55); [Luo et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib25)). In self-referential agents, one program both solves
tasks and improves itself: STOP runs a program improver on its own
code ([Zelikman et al., 2024](#bib.bib54)), Gödel Agent modifies its own
logic ([Yin et al., 2025](#bib.bib50)), the Darwin Gödel Machine lets
coding agents modify their own code ([Zhang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib56)),
and Hyperagents place a task agent and a meta agent in one editable
program, so the meta agent can rewrite itself ([Zhang et al.,
2026c](#bib.bib57)).

Other systems keep the optimizer separate and revise its procedure. Some
add a meta-level above it: a meta-evolution loop over the evolution
blueprint ([Seong et al., 2026](#bib.bib34)), a meta-agent that edits
the procedure controlling future evolution ([Zhang et al.,
2026d](#bib.bib58)) or a base agent’s context-engineering skills ([Ye
et al., 2026](#bib.bib49)), a meta-evolver that improves a harness
evolver’s strategy ([Zhou, 2026](#bib.bib59)), and a meta-level policy
that schedules improvement operators ([Tan et al., 2026](#bib.bib38)).
In others, the optimizer revises its own procedure. Promptbreeder
evolves the mutation prompts that improve its task prompts ([Fernando
et al., 2024](#bib.bib11)), SePO optimizes a prompt agent’s own system
prompt ([Tao et al., 2026](#bib.bib39)), MetaSkill-Evolve applies its
improvement pipeline to its own meta-skill ([Wang et al.,
2026c](#bib.bib43)), and EvoTrainer’s trainer revises its own harness
across training runs ([Chen et al., 2026a](#bib.bib7)).

Position of VERSE. Like the last group, the VERSE optimizer revises its
own procedure, but its harness $`g`$ is a full agent harness kept
separate from the executor harness $`h`$: it rewrites its own prompts,
skills, notes, hooks, and executable tools. While editing $`h`$, it
calls verification tools that test the causal claim behind an edit
before submission; AutoSaddler, HarnessEvolve, and EnvHarness also
verify edits, with fixed optimization procedures. The executor model
stays frozen and is evaluated on disjoint task splits. In our controlled
study, self-evolution lowered accuracy without verification and gave the
best result with it
(Section [2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Two Observations on Self-Evolving Optimizers ‣ 2 Setting and Motivating Observations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")).

## Appendix B Pseudocode for the bilevel learning protocol

A harness is a complete executable configuration, including prompts,
tools, control flow, dependencies, and persistent state. The executor
weights $`\theta`$ and optimizer weights $`\omega`$ remain fixed; $`h`$
controls task execution and $`g`$ controls candidate generation and
checking. All tasks, trajectories, harnesses, and evidence records have
finite encodings. Their spaces are countable, with all subsets
measurable; $`\mathcal{H}`$ denotes the space of executor harnesses.
Learning $`g`$ treats the optimizer as a learned algorithm ([Zakerinia
et al., 2024](#bib.bib53)), using execution feedback for program
revision ([Agrawal et al., 2026](#bib.bib1)).

The inner loop compares the nonempty candidate set
$`\mathcal{C}_{t}(g)`$ on the same training tasks; if a prescribed
training subset is used, the comparison uses its mean loss. The outer
objective conditions on the current state and task sets and averages
over candidate generation and evaluation. Candidates may run in
parallel, and their results need not be independent.

1: Fixed executor weights $`\theta`$ and optimizer weights $`\omega`$;
training $`S_{\mathrm{train}}`$,

2:   validation $`S_{\mathrm{val}}`$, and test $`S_{\mathrm{test}}`$;
initial $`h_{0},g_{0}`$;

3:   round count $`T\geq 1`$, candidates per round $`M\geq 1`$, budgets
$`B_{t}`$

4: Frozen executor harness $`\widehat{h}`$ and its test evaluation

5: Evaluate $`h_{0}`$ on $`S_{\mathrm{val}}`$ and archive the harness
and result

6: for $`t=0,\ldots,T-1`$ do $`\triangleright`$ Outer loop: improve the
optimizer

7:   Fix $`g_{t}`$; $`\mathcal{C}_{t}\leftarrow\varnothing`$; training
record $`E_{t}\leftarrow\varnothing`$

8:   for $`c=1,\ldots,M`$ do $`\triangleright`$ Inner loop: search for
executor harnesses

9:    $`\widetilde{h}\leftarrow h_{t}`$

10:    while the candidate’s turn limit is not reached do

11:      Read training traces, earlier validation feedback $`F_{<t}`$,
and prior evidence under $`g_{t}`$

12:      Propose an edit to $`\widetilde{h}`$ or a diagnostic check on
$`S_{\mathrm{train}}`$

13:      if a check is requested and the round’s shared budget remains
then

14:       Execute the check; append raw outcomes and costs to $`E_{t}`$

15:      end if

16:      Revise $`\widetilde{h}`$ using the available evidence

17:    end while

18:    $`\mathcal{C}_{t}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{t}\cup\{\widetilde{h}\}`$

19:   end for

20:   Evaluate $`\mathcal{C}_{t}`$ on the training comparison tasks

21:   Choose
$`h_{t+1}\in\argmin_{h\in\mathcal{C}_{t}}\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{train}}(h)`$,
or keep $`h_{t}`$ if $`\mathcal{C}_{t}=\varnothing`$

22:   Smoke-check $`h_{t+1}`$ and let the optimizer repair it if needed;
record results in $`E_{t}`$

23:   Evaluate $`h_{t+1}`$ on $`S_{\mathrm{val}}`$ to obtain feedback
$`F_{t}`$; archive both

24:   Use the optimizer to revise $`g_{t}`$ from $`E_{t},F_{t}`$,
obtaining $`g_{t+1}`$

25: end for

26: Re-evaluate the two archived harnesses with the lowest validation
losses; keep the better as $`\widehat{h}`$

27: Freeze $`\widehat{h}`$ and evaluate it on $`S_{\mathrm{test}}`$

28: return $`\widehat{h}`$ and its test evaluation

Algorithm 1 Verified self-evolving harness learning

Evidence and feedback. $`E_{t}`$ contains the training checks and
comparison results of round $`t`$. $`F_{t}`$ is the validation feedback
returned to the optimizer, the same for every method: the validation
loss of each round, the identifiers of up to 30 validation tasks whose
outcome changed and whether each change held on a rerun, and the rate
and two most frequent messages of harness-code errors on validation
runs. It never contains task statements, tests, or trajectories. Test
tasks enter neither loop.

Checks. All candidates and the optimizer self-update draw checks from
one execution budget per round, and every check runs on training tasks.
A check that cannot run, because the round’s budget or time is spent or
the draft cannot be applied, returns an inconclusive result; a draft
that cannot be applied costs no budget. No check result blocks a
submission: the optimizer decides whether to revise, check again, or
submit.

Choosing the harness of a round. The candidates are compared by the net
number of training tasks they fix on a shared subset of up to 12
training tasks. A tie goes to the candidate whose submitted edit has
more tasks that the verification tool confirmed as passing, and then to
a fixed order. The chosen candidate must pass a smoke check that
includes one run on a training task. If it fails, the optimizer repairs
it in at most three attempts, and the repaired version is validated and
archived as $`h_{t+1}`$ without being screened again. If no attempt
passes, or if no candidate was submitted, the round keeps $`h_{t}`$ and
skips validation; the self-update still runs.

Validation and final selection. A validation task whose outcome differs
from that of the parent harness is rerun once, and a change that does
not reproduce is reverted to the parent’s outcome. At the end, the two
archived harnesses with the lowest validation losses are each evaluated
once more, and the one with the lower mean loss is selected; ties favor
the later round.
Appendix [C.3](#A3.SS3 "C.3 Selection error ‣ Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
analyzes this rule and the reliability of validation losses under
feedback.

Self-updates. A self-update of the optimizer harness takes effect only
if its code passes a safety check and a test run; otherwise the previous
version stays in place.

## Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis

This appendix gives the formal versions of
Theorems [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Repair choice). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
and [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Finding and selecting a good harness). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
and the analysis of selection. Tasks, trajectories, harnesses, and
evidence have finite encodings, so all spaces are countable and all
subsets measurable. Logarithms are natural.

Tools of the optimizer. All methods in our experiments share the basic
tools $`\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{basic}}`$ of
equation [1](#S3.E1 "In 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses").
They read and search trajectories, run shell commands for analysis,
write notes that carry over to later rounds, and submit proposed edits
to the executor harness. Attribution and the training audit are not in
$`\mathcal{T}_{0}`$; they run automatically each round and add their
reports to the optimizer’s evidence. Self-evolving optimizers also keep
simple verification
(Section [2.3](#S2.SS3 "2.3 Two Observations on Self-Evolving Optimizers ‣ 2 Setting and Motivating Observations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")),
which the verification tool generalizes to up to three tasks; their
self-written tools can call both. Later optimizer harnesses keep all
tools in $`\mathcal{T}_{0}`$, can add new tools built from them, and can
revise the other parts of $`g`$.

### C.1 Choosing a repair from verification evidence

#### C.1.1 Checks as an adaptive hypothesis test

Fix two distinct repairs $`h_{1},h_{2}`$ for two possible causes
$`W\in\{1,2\}`$ with prior probabilities $`\lambda,1-\lambda`$,
$`\lambda\in[0,1]`$. The optimizer starts from the evidence
$`\mathcal{I}_{0}`$ gathered before any check, such as trajectories,
attribution reports, earlier feedback, and notes, and may make up to
$`n`$ checks. Check $`j`$ consists of a query $`Q_{j}`$ and feedback
$`Y_{j}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
Q_{j}\sim\Pi_{j}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{I}_{j-1}),\qquad Y_{j}\sim\kappa_{W}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{I}_{j-1},Q_{j}),\qquad\mathcal{I}_{j}=(\mathcal{I}_{j-1},Q_{j},Y_{j}).
``` |  | (5) |

The query policy $`\Pi_{j}`$ belongs to the optimizer. It may depend on
the whole history, including revisions of the optimizer harness, but not
on $`W`$, which the optimizer cannot observe. The feedback law
$`\kappa_{w}`$ belongs to the sandbox and may differ between causes. A
run that stops early makes null checks with constant feedback until
$`n`$; they carry no information.

For probability masses $`P,P^{\prime}`$ on a countable set, the
Bhattacharyya coefficient is
$`\operatorname{Aff}(P,P^{\prime})=\sum_{y}\sqrt{P(y)P^{\prime}(y)}`$ ([Bhattacharyya,
1943](#bib.bib5)). It lies in $`[0,1]`$, equals $`1`$ for identical
distributions, and equals $`0`$ for disjoint supports. The coefficient
of check $`j`$ is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\rho_{j}=\sup_{\mathcal{I}}\sum_{q}\Pi_{j}(q\mid\mathcal{I})\,\operatorname{Aff}\!\left(\kappa_{1}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{I},q),\kappa_{2}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{I},q)\right),
``` |  | (6) |

where the supremum runs over histories at step $`j-1`$ with positive
probability under both causes, and $`\rho_{j}=0`$ if there are none. It
averages the ambiguity of the feedback over the checks the optimizer
chooses at each history. The sandbox determines
$`\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}`$ and the optimizer determines $`\Pi_{j}`$;
either can make $`\rho_{j}`$ small.

#### C.1.2 Proof of Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Repair choice). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")

###### Lemma C.1.1 (Bayes error and the Bhattacharyya coefficient).

Let $`P_{1},P_{2}`$ be the laws of the evidence under the two causes and
$`\beta=\operatorname{Aff}(P_{1},P_{2})`$. The smallest error
probability of any decision rule,
$`p^{*}=\sum_{\mathcal{I}}\min\{\lambda P_{1}(\mathcal{I}),(1-\lambda)P_{2}(\mathcal{I})\}`$,
satisfies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\lambda(1-\lambda)\beta^{2}\leq p^{*}\leq\sqrt{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\,\beta\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\beta.
``` |  |

The upper bound is the Bhattacharyya bound on the Bayes error and the
lower bound is due to [Kailath (1967)](#bib.bib18); [Nielsen
(2014)](#bib.bib30) presents both.

###### Proof.

The rule that picks the cause with the larger posterior mass at each
$`\mathcal{I}`$ leaves the smaller mass as its error, and no rule,
randomized or not, does better pointwise. Write
$`a=\lambda P_{1}(\mathcal{I})`$ and
$`b=(1-\lambda)P_{2}(\mathcal{I})`$, so that $`\sum\min(a,b)=p^{*}`$,
$`\sum\max(a,b)=1-p^{*}`$, and
$`\sum\sqrt{ab}=\sqrt{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\,\beta`$. The upper bounds
follow from $`\min(a,b)\leq\sqrt{ab}`$ and
$`\lambda(1-\lambda)\leq\tfrac{1}{4}`$. For the lower bound,
$`\sqrt{ab}=\sqrt{\min(a,b)\max(a,b)}`$ and the Cauchy–Schwarz
inequality give
$`\lambda(1-\lambda)\beta^{2}\leq p^{*}(1-p^{*})\leq p^{*}`$. ∎

###### Lemma C.1.2 (Each check multiplies the coefficient).

Let $`P_{w}^{(j)}`$ be the law of $`\mathcal{I}_{j}`$ under cause $`w`$
and $`\beta_{j}=\operatorname{Aff}(P_{1}^{(j)},P_{2}^{(j)})`$. Then
$`\beta_{j}\leq\rho_{j}\beta_{j-1}`$, and $`\beta_{j}=\beta_{j-1}`$ when
$`\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}`$ at check $`j`$.

###### Proof.

Under
equation [5](#A3.E5 "In C.1.1 Checks as an adaptive hypothesis test ‣ C.1 Choosing a repair from verification evidence ‣ Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
$`P_{w}^{(j)}(\mathcal{I},q,y)=P_{w}^{(j-1)}(\mathcal{I})\,\Pi_{j}(q\mid\mathcal{I})\,\kappa_{w}(y\mid\mathcal{I},q)`$,
and the factor $`\Pi_{j}`$ is the same under both causes. Hence

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\beta_{j}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{I}}\sqrt{P_{1}^{(j-1)}(\mathcal{I})P_{2}^{(j-1)}(\mathcal{I})}\sum_{q}\Pi_{j}(q\mid\mathcal{I})\sum_{y}\sqrt{\kappa_{1}(y\mid\mathcal{I},q)\kappa_{2}(y\mid\mathcal{I},q)}\leq\rho_{j}\beta_{j-1},`$ |  | (7) |

because the inner double sum is at most $`\rho_{j}`$ at every history
with positive mass under both causes, and a history with zero mass under
either cause contributes nothing. If $`\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}`$, the
innermost sum equals $`1`$ and the expression equals $`\beta_{j-1}`$. ∎

Theorem
[1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Repair choice). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
(formal). Let
$`p_{n}^{*}=\inf_{\psi}\mathbb{P}\{\psi(\mathcal{I}_{n})\neq h_{W}\}`$
over decision rules $`\psi`$ on complete histories. Then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p_{n}^{*}\leq\sqrt{\lambda(1-\lambda)}\,\beta_{0}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\rho_{j}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{0}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\rho_{j},
``` |  | (8) |

and if $`\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}`$ at every check, then
$`p_{n}^{*}\geq\lambda(1-\lambda)\beta_{0}^{2}`$. The optimizer’s choice
$`\widehat{h}`$, which may use the history and private randomness, errs
with probability $`p_{n}^{*}+\varepsilon`$ for some
$`\varepsilon\geq 0`$.

###### Proof.

Lemma [C.1.2](#A3.SS1.Thmlemma2 "Lemma C.1.2 (Each check multiplies the coefficient). ‣ C.1.2 Proof of Theorem ‣ C.1 Choosing a repair from verification evidence ‣ Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
gives $`\beta_{n}\leq\beta_{0}\prod_{j}\rho_{j}`$, with
$`\beta_{n}=\beta_{0}`$ when no check depends on the cause.
Lemma [C.1.1](#A3.SS1.Thmlemma1 "Lemma C.1.1 (Bayes error and the Bhattacharyya coefficient). ‣ C.1.2 Proof of Theorem ‣ C.1 Choosing a repair from verification evidence ‣ Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
applied to the laws of $`\mathcal{I}_{n}`$ gives both bounds, and
$`\varepsilon\geq 0`$ because $`p_{n}^{*}`$ is the minimum over all
rules. ∎

The lower bound holds for every query policy, hence for every optimizer
harness. A check that only re-analyzes the recorded evidence, such as
re-reading traces or running static analysis over them, has
$`\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}`$ and leaves the coefficient unchanged. A check
can lower it only if its feedback distinguishes the causes beyond the
existing evidence; executing a draft repair provides such feedback. For
example, perturbation can replace a command whose arguments may be wrong
and replay the remaining steps.

The confirmation rerun of the verification tool lowers the coefficient
further. Consider a check that runs the draft repair $`h_{1}`$ on a
target training task, and let it pass with probability $`q_{1}`$ when
cause $`1`$ is present and $`q_{2}`$ otherwise. The coefficient of this
check is $`\rho=\sqrt{q_{1}q_{2}}+\sqrt{(1-q_{1})(1-q_{2})}`$. The
verification tool reruns the task after a pass. With independent runs
the outcomes are a fail, a pass followed by a fail, and two passes, and
the coefficient becomes

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sqrt{(1-q_{1})(1-q_{2})}+\sqrt{q_{1}q_{2}}\,\rho=\rho-\sqrt{q_{1}q_{2}}\,(1-\rho),
``` |  |

which is below $`\rho`$ whenever $`q_{1}q_{2}>0`$ and $`\rho<1`$. A
wrong repair that passes by chance with probability $`q_{2}`$ passes
twice with probability $`q_{2}^{2}`$.

### C.2 Finding a good harness

#### C.2.1 Conditions and proof of Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Finding and selecting a good harness). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")

Write $`L(h)=L_{\theta,D}(h)`$ for a fixed executor and task
distribution; expectations run over the whole run. The run makes $`K`$
proposals in a fixed order; $`K=TM`$ in
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Appendix B Pseudocode for the bilevel learning protocol ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
where the candidates of one round may run in parallel and their outcomes
may be dependent. Let $`h^{(i)}`$ be the harness submitted by proposal
$`i`$, repaired if it fails the smoke check
(Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Pseudocode for the bilevel learning protocol ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")),
and let $`\mathcal{C}=\{h_{0},h^{(1)},\ldots,h^{(K)}\}`$. If proposal
$`i`$ produces no candidate, $`h^{(i)}`$ is the current harness, so such
failures only lower $`r_{i}`$. The selected harness $`\widehat{h}`$ lies
in $`\mathcal{C}`$.

Fix $`\ell_{0}\in[0,1]`$. Let $`A_{i}`$ be the event that $`h_{0}`$ and
$`h^{(1)},\ldots,h^{(i)}`$ all have loss above $`\ell_{0}`$, with
$`A_{0}=\{L(h_{0})>\ell_{0}\}`$. Let $`O_{i}`$ be the event that
proposal $`i`$ targets a failure whose correct repair has loss at most
$`\ell_{0}`$, and $`J_{i}`$ the event that it submits that repair, so
that $`O_{i}\cap J_{i}\subseteq\{L(h^{(i)})\leq\ell_{0}\}`$. The
conditions of
Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Finding and selecting a good harness). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
are, for constants $`r_{i},e_{i}\in[0,1]`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}(O_{i}\mid A_{i-1})\geq r_{i},\qquad\mathbb{P}(J_{i}^{c}\mid O_{i},A_{i-1})\leq e_{i},
``` |  | (9) |

whenever the conditioning events have positive probability; if
$`\mathbb{P}(O_{i}\mid A_{i-1})=0`$ we set $`r_{i}=0`$. These conditions
allow proposals to depend on earlier outcomes and on revisions of the
optimizer harness; independence between proposals is not required. The
repair must lower the population loss, not only fix the training task it
was found on.

Theorem
[2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Finding and selecting a good harness). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
(formal). Under
equation [9](#A3.E9 "In C.2.1 Conditions and proof of Theorem ‣ C.2 Finding a good harness ‣ Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
with
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}=\mathbb{E}[L(\widehat{h})-\min_{h\in\mathcal{C}}L(h)]`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}L(\widehat{h})\leq\ell_{0}+(1-\ell_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{K}[1-r_{i}(1-e_{i})]+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}.
``` |  | (10) |

If $`r_{i}\geq r`$ and $`e_{i}\leq e`$ for all $`i`$, the middle term is
at most $`(1-\ell_{0})[1-r(1-e)]^{K}`$.

###### Proof.

When $`\mathbb{P}(A_{i-1})>0`$ and $`\mathbb{P}(O_{i}\mid A_{i-1})>0`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}\{L(h^{(i)})\leq\ell_{0}\mid A_{i-1}\}\geq\mathbb{P}(O_{i}\mid A_{i-1})\,\mathbb{P}(J_{i}\mid O_{i},A_{i-1})\geq r_{i}(1-e_{i}),
``` |  |

and the bound also holds when either probability is zero. Since
$`A_{i}=A_{i-1}\cap\{L(h^{(i)})>\ell_{0}\}`$, this gives
$`\mathbb{P}(A_{i})\leq[1-r_{i}(1-e_{i})]\mathbb{P}(A_{i-1})`$, and
iterating from $`\mathbb{P}(A_{0})\leq 1`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}\Bigl\{\min_{h\in\mathcal{C}}L(h)>\ell_{0}\Bigr\}=\mathbb{P}(A_{K})\leq\prod_{i=1}^{K}[1-r_{i}(1-e_{i})].
``` |  |

The minimum loss is at most $`\ell_{0}`$ off $`A_{K}`$ and at most $`1`$
on it, so
$`\mathbb{E}\min_{\mathcal{C}}L\leq\ell_{0}+(1-\ell_{0})\mathbb{P}(A_{K})`$.
Adding $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}`$ proves the claim. ∎

#### C.2.2 Connecting verification to the repair error

Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Repair choice). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
bounds $`e_{i}`$ when the repair decision of proposal $`i`$ is a test
between two causes. Condition on $`O_{i}`$, $`A_{i-1}`$, and the
proposal’s context, and suppose that within this context the optimizer
runs the adaptive test of
Appendix [C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Choosing a repair from verification evidence ‣ Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
with at most $`n_{i}`$ checks and submits the repair it selects. Let
$`\rho_{i,j}`$ bound the coefficient of check $`j`$ and
$`\varepsilon_{i}`$ the optimizer’s excess error, uniformly over
contexts. Applying
Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1 (Repair choice). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
in each context and averaging gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}(J_{i}^{c}\mid O_{i},A_{i-1})\leq e_{i}:=\min\!\left\{1,\frac{1}{2}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\rho_{i,j}+\varepsilon_{i}\right\},
``` |  | (11) |

which can be substituted into
equation [10](#A3.E10 "In C.2.1 Conditions and proof of Theorem ‣ C.2 Finding a good harness ‣ Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses").

If every proposal makes $`n`$ checks with coefficient at most $`\rho<1`$
and excess error at most $`\varepsilon`$, then
$`e_{i}\leq\min\{1,\tfrac{1}{2}\rho^{n}+\varepsilon\}`$ for every $`i`$.
The first term decays geometrically in the number of checks, so a few
checks bring the bound close to $`\varepsilon`$; further gains come from
lower $`\varepsilon`$ and higher $`r_{i}`$, which self-evolution can act
on.

The two factors of $`r_{i}(1-e_{i})`$ multiply. Increasing $`r_{i}`$ by
$`\Delta`$ raises the lower bound on the success probability of a
proposal by only $`\Delta(1-e_{i})`$, so better target selection helps
little while repairs often fail. Within each benchmark, all methods use
the same $`K`$
(Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")),
so in
equation [4](#S3.E4 "In Theorem 2 (Finding and selecting a good harness). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
they differ only through $`r_{i}`$, $`e_{i}`$, and
$`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}`$.

### C.3 Selection error

#### C.3.1 Two stages

Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Finding and selecting a good harness). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
leaves the selection error $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}`$: even when a
good harness has been proposed, the run must recognize it from measured
losses. Selection has two stages
(Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Appendix B Pseudocode for the bilevel learning protocol ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")).
Within a round, the candidate with the lowest training loss is kept, and
these candidates together with $`h_{0}`$ form the archive
$`\mathcal{V}`$; at the end, the archived harness with the lowest
validation loss is selected. The first stage uses tasks whose
trajectories the optimizer has read; the second uses tasks of which it
has seen only validation losses and outcome summaries. The lemma charges
each stage for its estimation error.

###### Lemma C.3.1 (Selection in two stages).

Suppose that with probability at least $`1-\delta`$, every training loss
is within $`a_{\mathrm{train}}`$ of its candidate’s population loss and
every validation loss on $`\mathcal{V}`$ is within $`a_{\mathrm{val}}`$.
Then, with $`c=\min\{1,2a_{\mathrm{train}}+2a_{\mathrm{val}}\}`$, we
have $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}\leq c+(1-c)\delta`$.

###### Proof.

Let $`h_{*}`$ minimize $`L`$ on $`\mathcal{C}`$. On the stated event, if
$`h_{*}`$ was proposed in a round whose kept candidate is
$`h^{\mathrm{scr}}`$, then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
L(h^{\mathrm{scr}})\leq\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{train}}(h^{\mathrm{scr}})+a_{\mathrm{train}}\leq\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{train}}(h_{*})+a_{\mathrm{train}}\leq L(h_{*})+2a_{\mathrm{train}},
``` |  |

and if $`h_{*}=h_{0}`$ it is in $`\mathcal{V}`$ already; so
$`\min_{\mathcal{V}}L\leq\min_{\mathcal{C}}L+2a_{\mathrm{train}}`$. The
same comparison for validation selection on $`\mathcal{V}`$ gives
$`L(\widehat{h})\leq\min_{\mathcal{V}}L+2a_{\mathrm{val}}`$. The gap
$`L(\widehat{h})-\min_{\mathcal{C}}L`$ is therefore at most $`c`$ on the
event and at most $`1`$ off it; take expectations. ∎

This is the usual comparison between empirical and population
minimizers ([Hastie et al., 2009](#bib.bib12)), applied twice. It also
covers the final step of the protocol, which re-evaluates the two
archived harnesses with the lowest validation losses and keeps the one
with the lower mean: the minimizer of $`L`$ on $`\mathcal{V}`$ is either
among the two, and then the winner’s mean loss is no larger than its
own, or it is not, and then both finalists have first losses no larger
than its own. In both cases
$`L(\widehat{h})\leq\min_{\mathcal{V}}L+2a_{\mathrm{val}}`$ as long as
first losses and mean losses are within $`a_{\mathrm{val}}`$ of the
population loss. The optimizer builds its candidates from the training
trajectories, so $`a_{\mathrm{train}}`$ is a condition on the training
comparison rather than a consequence of sampling. In our implementation,
a candidate that fails the smoke check is repaired before validation
without being screened again
(Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Pseudocode for the bilevel learning protocol ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"));
such repairs address loading and runtime errors. The lemma covers this
case with the repaired version as the candidate in $`\mathcal{C}`$ and
its training loss measured before the repair. The condition on
$`a_{\mathrm{train}}`$ then compares this measured loss with the
population loss of the repaired version. The next subsection bounds
$`a_{\mathrm{val}}`$.

#### C.3.2 Validation losses under feedback

The optimizer never executes validation tasks, but their losses and
outcome summaries are returned to it and shape later candidates. Each
round therefore chooses a harness after looking at the validation set
through this feedback. This is adaptive data analysis: how much the
validation loss can flatter the chosen harness is governed by how much
the feedback reveals, and can be bounded by the number of possible
values of the feedback ([Dwork et al., 2015](#bib.bib10); [Blum & Hardt,
2015](#bib.bib6)) or by its entropy ([Russo & Zou, 2016](#bib.bib33);
[Xu & Raginsky, 2017](#bib.bib47)). [Bertran et al. (2026)](#bib.bib4)
apply the same argument to research agents that receive one bit of
validation feedback per submission. We give both forms.

Let $`X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}\sim D`$ be the validation tasks and, for each
harness $`h`$ and run index $`r`$, let
$`Y_{i}^{(r)}(h)\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid X_{i};h)`$ be a complete
trajectory; given the task, reruns are independent. Collect everything
about task $`i`$ in $`Z_{i}=(X_{i},(Y_{i}^{(r)}(h))_{h,r})`$. The
$`Z_{i}`$ are i.i.d. and independent of $`U`$, the training data and the
random seeds that drive the optimizer and every training-side execution.
A validation estimate is an average
$`\widehat{\phi}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i}\phi(Z_{i})`$ of a per-task
statistic $`\phi(Z_{i})\in[0,1]`$ with mean $`\bar{\phi}`$. The
validation loss of $`h`$ uses one run per task,
$`\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{val}}(h)=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i}\ell(X_{i},Y_{i}^{(1)}(h))`$,
and has mean $`L(h)`$.

Let $`F=(F_{0},\ldots,F_{T-1})`$ be the feedback returned across rounds.
We assume that $`F`$ takes values in a finite set $`\mathcal{F}`$, as it
does when every summary has bounded length. Our summaries list at most
30 task identifiers and the two most frequent error messages. The number
of possible summaries can still be large, and the bounds below are
informative only when $`\log|\mathcal{F}|`$ is small relative to $`m`$.
Given $`U`$ and $`F_{0},\ldots,F_{t-1}`$, the run is deterministic up to
the validation of round $`t`$: the validated harness, the statistics
that evaluate it, and the optimizer harness $`g_{t}`$ are all functions
of the feedback so far.

###### Proposition C.3.2 (Uniform deviation of validation estimates).

Let each validated harness receive at most $`R`$ estimates. For
$`0<\delta<1`$, with probability at least $`1-\delta`$ every estimate
the run computes satisfies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|\widehat{\phi}-\bar{\phi}|\leq\varepsilon_{m}=\sqrt{\frac{\log|\mathcal{F}|+\log(2R(T+1)/\delta)}{2m}},
``` |  |

and so does every average of such estimates.

###### Proof.

Condition on $`U=u`$; the $`Z_{i}`$ keep their law. The harness of round
$`t\in\{0,\ldots,T\}`$, where round $`0`$ validates $`h_{0}`$, and each
of its $`R`$ statistics are fixed once $`F_{0},\ldots,F_{t-1}`$ are, and
this prefix takes at most $`|\mathcal{F}|`$ values. So at most
$`R(T+1)|\mathcal{F}|`$ statistics, each fixed before the $`Z_{i}`$ are
drawn, can ever be computed. Hoeffding’s inequality ([Hoeffding,
1963](#bib.bib14)) gives
$`\mathbb{P}(|\widehat{\phi}-\bar{\phi}|>\varepsilon)\leq 2e^{-2m\varepsilon^{2}}`$
for each, and a union bound at $`\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{m}`$ leaves
failure probability $`\delta`$. Averages inherit the bound by the
triangle inequality; integrate over $`u`$. ∎

###### Proposition C.3.3 (Expected optimism).

Let $`\widehat{h}`$ be an archived harness chosen by any rule. Then,
with entropies in nats,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}\bigl[L(\widehat{h})-\widehat{L}_{\mathrm{val}}(\widehat{h})\bigr]\leq\sqrt{\frac{H(F\mid U)+\log(T+1)}{2m}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log|\mathcal{F}|+\log(T+1)}{2m}}.
``` |  |

###### Proof.

Condition on $`U=u`$. The archive is a function of $`F`$ and
$`\widehat{h}`$ is its entry at some index $`J\in\{0,\ldots,T\}`$, so
$`H(\widehat{h}\mid U=u)\leq H(F\mid U=u)+\log(T+1)`$. The loss
$`\ell(X_{i},Y_{i}^{(1)}(h))\in[0,1]`$ is $`\tfrac{1}{2}`$-sub-Gaussian
for every $`h`$, and $`\widehat{h}`$ is a function of $`Z_{1:m}`$.
Theorem 1 of [Xu & Raginsky (2017)](#bib.bib47) with
$`\sigma=\tfrac{1}{2}`$ bounds the expected gap by
$`\sqrt{I(Z_{1:m};\widehat{h}\mid U=u)/(2m)}`$, and
$`I(Z_{1:m};\widehat{h}\mid U=u)\leq H(\widehat{h}\mid U=u)`$. Average
over $`u`$ with Jensen’s inequality, and use
$`H(F\mid U)\leq\log|\mathcal{F}|`$. ∎

Both bounds depend on the feedback, not on the harness. The size and
content of what the optimizer writes do not enter them, and the
optimizer harness $`g_{t}`$ is itself a function of $`U`$ and the
feedback so far, so both loops are covered by the same feedback. What
matters is $`\log|\mathcal{F}|`$. With no feedback it is $`0`$ and only
the choice among $`T+1`$ archived harnesses remains; a validation loss
per round contributes at most $`\log(m+1)`$; an improved-or-not bit
contributes $`\log 2`$; a list of which tasks changed outcome
contributes up to $`m\log 3`$; and returning validation trajectories
would make $`|\mathcal{F}|`$ far larger. This is the trade-off behind
the protocol of
Section [2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Training, validation, and test splits ‣ 2 Setting and Motivating Observations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses").
Feedback can raise $`r_{i}`$ and lower $`e_{i}`$ in
Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2 (Finding and selecting a good harness). ‣ 3.2 Theoretical Analysis ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
by showing which edits held up on unseen tasks, and it enlarges
$`a_{\mathrm{val}}`$ through $`\log|\mathcal{F}|`$; restricting its
possible values lowers this cost.

The protocol of
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Pseudocode for the bilevel learning protocol ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
also reruns a validation task whose outcome differs from the parent’s
and keeps the change only if it reproduces. Such a confirmed estimate is
an average of per-task statistics and is covered by
Proposition [C.3.2](#A3.SS3.Thmlemma2 "Proposition C.3.2 (Uniform deviation of validation estimates). ‣ C.3.2 Validation losses under feedback ‣ C.3 Selection error ‣ Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
but its mean is not exactly $`L(h)`$. On a task where $`h`$ fails with
probability $`p`$, let $`a`$ be its first outcome, $`b`$ the parent’s,
and $`r`$ the rerun; the confirmed outcome equals $`a`$ when $`a=b`$ and
$`r`$ otherwise, so
$`\mathbb{E}[c-a\mid x]=p\,\mathbb{P}(a=0,b=1)-(1-p)\,\mathbb{P}(a=1,b=0)`$,
and both terms lie in $`[0,p(1-p)]`$. The mean of a confirmed estimate
is therefore within
$`v(h)=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim D}[p_{h}(x)(1-p_{h}(x))]\leq\tfrac{1}{4}`$ of
$`L(h)`$, with $`v(h)=0`$ when outcomes are deterministic given the
task. In
Lemma [C.3.1](#A3.SS3.Thmlemma1 "Lemma C.3.1 (Selection in two stages). ‣ C.3.1 Two stages ‣ C.3 Selection error ‣ Appendix C Additional theoretical analysis ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
this term adds to $`a_{\mathrm{val}}`$: with the two estimates per
harness that the final step uses, $`a_{\mathrm{val}}=\varepsilon_{m}`$
at $`R=2`$ plus $`\max_{h}v(h)`$.

## Appendix D Additional experiment details

### D.1 Data and grading

SWE-rebench splits. The training and validation pools draw from the
monthly leaderboard pool of the benchmark. The test split was frozen
separately, before any method development, and an n-gram overlap scan
between the training or validation task texts and the test tasks found
no contamination. The sealed test pool holds 110 tasks. Two of them fail
their own official reference solution under our grading pipeline and are
excluded, so in-distribution SWE-rebench accuracies use 108 tasks.

Out-of-distribution test set. The OOD test set of
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4.2 Main results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
is the July 2026 release of the SWE-rebench leaderboard, which covers
tasks from May 15 to July 1, 2026. It holds 111 tasks in Go (21), Java
(21), Python (20), Rust (25), and TypeScript (24). Four Java tasks fail
their reference solution in our environment and are excluded, so OOD
accuracies use 107 tasks: Go 21, Java 17, Python 20, Rust 25, and
TypeScript 24. On the 87 tasks outside Python, blank scores $`25.3\%`$,
and VERSE with self-evolution scores $`35.6\%`$ on the Meta-Harness host
and $`37.9\%`$ on the HarnessX host, the two hosts with the largest OOD
gains in
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4.2 Main results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses").
Three of the 20 Python tasks come from a repository that also appears in
the training and validation sets; the in-distribution test set shares no
repository with them.

Terminal-Bench splits. The Terminal-Bench ([Merrill et al.,
2026](#bib.bib27)) pools follow the suite generations, which share no
tasks. Training uses 104 Terminal-Bench 1.x tasks, and the test split is
the 89 tasks of Terminal-Bench 2.x at a pinned commit, so training never
sees a test environment. The validation pool holds 98 tasks (20 from
Terminal-Bench 1.x and 78 from Terminal-Bench-Lite), and each run
samples 50 of them with a fixed seed.

Grading. Each task is graded by its own test script, and task
environments are frozen as image snapshots, so the checker version
cannot drift during a run. Every validation task passed a screening
check that its official reference solution passes our grading pipeline.
Grader logs are available on training tasks only.

### D.2 Models and budgets

Models.
Sections [2](#S2 "2 Setting and Motivating Observations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
and [4](#S4 "4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
use Qwen3.8-Flash-Next as the optimizer and Qwen3.8-27B as the executor,
served through vLLM; the executor runs with low reasoning effort and
greedy decoding. The appendix also reports results with closed-weight
models: the small tier of an anonymized model family as the executor and
its mid-size tier as the optimizer. We refer to them as the small model
and the mid-size model. These models satisfy the two conditions [Wang
et al. (2026b)](#bib.bib42) identify for informative harness evolution
studies: the executor has headroom, and performance depends strongly on
the harness. Observation 2
(Figures [3](#S3.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 3.1 Overview of VERSE ‣ 3 VERSE ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
and [13](#A11.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ Appendix K Additional results for Observation 2 ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"))
also uses four further open-weight executors: Qwen3-Coder-480B, DeepSeek
V3.2, Mistral Large 3, and GLM-5. The first two also serve as target
executors in the generalization study of
Appendix [G](#A7 "Appendix G Generalization study ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses").

Budget. Every method runs $`T=6`$ evolution rounds on SWE-rebench and
$`T=3`$ on Terminal-Bench, with three candidates generated per round,
the same proposal-turn limit, the same sandbox parallelism, and the same
harness edit space (executable hooks plus prompts). The only axis that
varies between methods is what the optimizer is given: the evidence
interface, the verification tools, and the right to edit its own
harness.

Simple verification budget. In
Table [1](#S2.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 2.2 Training, validation, and test splits ‣ 2 Setting and Motivating Observations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
simple verification executes one training task under an optional draft
edit in an isolated copy of the executor harness. We call each such
execution, and each call to a VERSE verification tool, a *probe*. Each
call returns the test verdict, the trajectory path, and the remaining
execution budget. The configured allowance is eight calls per candidate,
scaled by the three candidates to a shared pool of 24 calls per round.
The three candidates and the subsequent optimizer self-update draw from
this same pool. A passing probe does not automatically accept an edit;
the optimizer decides whether to revise, probe again, or submit, after
which the shared screening and validation procedure applies.

Candidate search and verification costs. Each candidate has at most 60
optimizer turns. The candidates are compared on a shared subset of up to
12 training tasks within that round; the same comparison procedure and
validation-selection rule apply to baselines and VERSE. A failed
generation can leave fewer than three candidates.

For variants with verification, the configured allowance is eight
experiment units per candidate, pooled into a shared limit of 24 units
per round. This is one common pool, not three independently enforced
limits: calls to the three verification tools and, for self-evolving
optimizers, to simple verification, including calls during the optimizer
self-update, draw from it. In the four self-evolving runs of
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4.2 Main results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
simple verification was called 21 times, against 140 calls to the three
verification tools. A verification call runs at most three target tasks
and costs one unit per target task; a replay or perturbation call costs
one unit. Units count experiments, not physical executions: a successful
verification call includes a confirmation rerun, and a perturbation call
can require both a baseline and a modified replay. All these calls use
$`S_{\mathrm{train}}`$ only.

Baselines without verification receive no probe allowance, and these
extra executions are not offset by reducing other runs. Automatic
attribution has a separate time budget; optimizer self-updates also
require additional model calls. Thus the comparisons match candidate
count, proposal-turn limits, and the evaluation protocol, but not total
execution or token cost;
Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3 "D.3 Cost of evolution ‣ Appendix D Additional experiment details ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
reports the cost of each run. Fixed and self-evolving variants with
verification share the same probe allowance.

### D.3 Cost of evolution

| Method | Agent runs | Verification | Units | Replays | Tokens | Opt. calls | Hours |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Meta-Harness ([Lee et al., 2026b](#bib.bib21)) | 1,326 | – | – | 0 | 550 | 1,030 | 21.6 |
| $`+`$ VERSE (w/o self-evolve) | 1,374 | 39 | 35 | 12 | 567 | 1,042 | 32.6 |
| $`+`$ VERSE (w/ self-evolve) | 1,453 | 95 | 67 | 20 | 634 | 1,126 | 38.0 |
| \[1.8pt/2.2pt\] AHE ([Lin et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)) | 1,150 | – | – | 0 | 378 | 1,035 | 15.3 |
| $`+`$ VERSE (w/o self-evolve) | 1,384 | 27 | 27 | 15 | 731 | 1,032 | 28.5 |
| $`+`$ VERSE (w/ self-evolve) | 1,399 | 44 | 43 | 19 | 562 | 715 | 23.8 |
| \[1.8pt/2.2pt\] Self-Harness ([Zhang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib55)) | 1,304 | – | – | 0 | 504 | 1,058 | 21.5 |
| $`+`$ VERSE (w/o self-evolve) | 1,404 | 33 | 33 | 16 | 628 | 1,042 | 28.3 |
| $`+`$ VERSE (w/ self-evolve) | 1,467 | 104 | 86 | 18 | 516 | 931 | 32.9 |
| \[1.8pt/2.2pt\] HarnessX ([Chen et al., 2026c](#bib.bib9)) | 1,035 | – | – | 0 | 443 | 1,030 | 19.0 |
| $`+`$ VERSE (w/o self-evolve) | 1,388 | 39 | 36 | 11 | 494 | 1,051 | 29.9 |
| $`+`$ VERSE (w/ self-evolve) | 1,441 | 70 | 73 | 37 | 700 | 900 | 47.0 |

Table 4: Cost of each evolution run of
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4.2 Main results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
counted from the run logs. *Agent runs*: runs of the executor on one
task, with model calls, excluding test evaluation; *Verification*: the
agent runs of the verification tools and simple verification, including
their confirmation reruns; *Units*: the verification budget spent;
*Replays*: re-executions of recorded commands without model calls;
*Tokens*: executor tokens in the training, screening, and validation
runs (millions); *Opt. calls*: optimizer model turns while generating
candidates and during self-updates; *Hours*: wall-clock time from the
start of round 1 to the end of harness selection.

Table [4](#A4.T4 "Table 4 ‣ D.3 Cost of evolution ‣ Appendix D Additional experiment details ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
reports what each run executed. An agent run is one run of the executor
on one task. Agent runs comprise the training, screening, and validation
runs of every round, the re-evaluation of the two finalists, the
confirmation reruns of validation changes, the smoke checks, and the
agent runs of the verification tools. Replays re-execute recorded
commands in a fresh container without calling a model; trace
minimization and the replay tool use one replay, and a perturbation call
uses two, one with the original step and one with the changed step.

Units count verification experiments rather than executions: a
verification call costs one unit per target task, but each target that
passes also gets a confirmation run. Optimizer tokens were not logged,
so the table counts optimizer model turns instead. Test evaluation is
the same for every method and adds three runs of the 108 in-distribution
and 107 out-of-distribution tasks for each evaluated harness.

Training, screening, and validation runs account for 91 to 98% of the
agent runs of every evolution run. The verification tools add 27 to 104
agent runs, 2 to 7% of a run’s agent runs, and at most 37 replays. The
larger gaps in agent runs between a baseline and its VERSE versions come
from rounds in which none of the baseline’s candidates could be applied,
so the round ran no screening or validation (AHE and HarnessX). The
self-evolving optimizer on the AHE host ends each candidate after a
median of 26 turns instead of 58, so it makes fewer optimizer calls than
the baseline despite its self-updates.

VERSE runs take 1.3 to 2.5 times as long as their baselines in
wall-clock time. Waiting for verification runs while candidates are
generated accounts for 1.0 to 4.7 hours of this. Most of the rest is
screening and validation, which take longer when an evolved harness
makes each executor run longer and when a baseline skipped rounds; the
load on the shared executor servers also differed between runs.

### D.4 Counting draft evaluations

The top panel of
Figure [5](#S4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 4.3 Ablations ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
and the draft counts of
Section [4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 What verification does during evolution ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
follow these rules. In the evolution runs of
Tables [1](#S2.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 2.2 Training, validation, and test splits ‣ 2 Setting and Motivating Observations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")–[5](#S4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 4.3 Ablations ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
the verification tool made 170 draft evaluations. Each run of the tool
counts as one evaluation, so a draft tested twice counts twice. The tool
reports a tested task as passed only after it passes twice. For each
passed task, we check whether the harness being edited passed it in its
own training run. This sorts the 170 evaluations into four groups:

- •
  in 17, the draft fixed a task that was failing;
- •
  in 30, it passed only on tasks that already passed, added by the
  optimizer as regression checks;
- •
  in 112, it passed none of its tasks;
- •
  11 were inconclusive: 10 inconclusive verdicts and one pass whose task
  is missing from the log.

Some drafts in the first two groups became the round’s harness
unchanged. Across these drafts, all 5 tasks they fixed and 11 of the 12
tasks they used as regression checks passed again in the next training
run. When we report how often optimizers revised a draft that passed
none of its tasks, a draft counts as revised if the final submission of
its candidate differs from it.

|  |  Optimizer implementation |  Optimizer model |  Evidence interface |  Edit check |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  Meta-Harness |  off-the-shelf coding agent (Claude Code) plus a small skill file |  Claude Opus 4.6 |  prior harnesses, scores, and raw traces, read through the filesystem |  none before submission; the next round scores the edit |
|  AHE |  hand-built pipeline (Evolve Agent in the authors’ NexAU framework) |  GPT-5.4 |  evidence corpus distilled from trajectories by an LLM debugger |  prediction logged at edit time, checked against the next round’s outcomes |
|  Self-Harness |  hand-built propose, evaluate, accept loop on the DeepAgents SDK |  same model as the executor |  deterministic clustering of failed traces into weakness patterns |  regression test gate run by the outer loop |
|  HarnessX |  hand-built four-stage workflow (Digester, Planner, Evolver, Critic) |  one meta agent LLM (not stated in the main text) |  on-demand trajectory summaries from the digester stage |  deterministic checks gate shipping |
|  VERSE (ours) |  agentic loop whose workspace and prompts are themselves optimized |  Qwen3.8-Flash-Next; mid-size model (Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Models and budgets ‣ Appendix D Additional experiment details ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")) |  raw traces plus attribution, trace minimization, and a training audit |  the verification tool executes the edit before submission, called by the optimizer itself |

Table 5: The four baselines as implemented in their original papers,
from the respective publications. In our experiments
(Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"))
all methods are reimplemented under one protocol: same executor, same
edit space, same candidate count and proposal-turn limit, and the same
optimizer model for every method; only the evidence interface of each
method is preserved.

## Appendix E Baseline reimplementation details

Table [5](#A4.T5 "Table 5 ‣ D.4 Counting draft evaluations ‣ Appendix D Additional experiment details ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
compares how the four published harness optimizers are implemented in
their original papers. All four keep the optimizer fixed while it
evolves the executor harness; they differ in how the optimizer is built,
which model powers it, and how an edit is checked. VERSE differs on the
two axes this paper studies: the optimizer harness is itself optimized,
and the execution check is a tool the optimizer calls before submitting
an edit, not an outer-loop gate applied afterwards.

Evidence interfaces. The four methods differ in the evidence interface
through which the optimizer digests failures: Meta-Harness reads raw
traces directly, AHE distills them into per-task root-cause reports,
Self-Harness mines model-specific weakness patterns, and HarnessX
summarizes trajectories on demand.

Evaluation protocols of the original papers. The original papers use
different evaluation protocols. Meta-Harness uses separate test sets in
several domains and reports its Terminal-Bench 2 results on the tasks
used for search ([Lee et al., 2026b](#bib.bib21)); AHE and HarnessX
report results on the task set used for evolution ([Lin et al.,
2026](#bib.bib23); [Chen et al., 2026c](#bib.bib9)); Self-Harness hides
held-out traces from the proposer and uses held-out scores to accept
edits ([Zhang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib55)). We therefore evaluate all
methods under the protocol of
Section [2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Training, validation, and test splits ‣ 2 Setting and Motivating Observations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses").

Our reimplementations preserve each method’s evidence interface, the
column that distinguishes the four systems in
Table [5](#A4.T5 "Table 5 ‣ D.4 Counting draft evaluations ‣ Appendix D Additional experiment details ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses").
Everything else is unified for the controlled comparison: all four run
inside our engine with the same executor and harness edit space
(executable hooks plus prompts), the same optimizer model, the same
candidate count and proposal-turn limit, the same round structure, the
same harness selection rule on the validation split, and the same
evaluation protocol
(Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Setup ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")).
The original systems use different executors, edit spaces, optimizer
models, acceptance rules, and evaluation protocols.

Section [2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Training, validation, and test splits ‣ 2 Setting and Motivating Observations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
explains how our shared data splits separate optimization feedback from
final test performance. Test-time scaling methods are not comparable
baselines under this protocol, because they produce per-instance
adaptations rather than a reusable harness.

three times for the four baselines and for VERSE on the HarnessX host.

Table 6: Each baseline equipped with VERSE, small and mid-size models
(avg@3 accuracy, %, $`\pm`$SEM over three test evaluations; one
evolution run per entry). “$`+`$ VERSE (w/o self-evolve)” adds the
verification tools to the baseline above; “w/ self-evolve” also lets the
optimizer edit its own harness. $`r^{\star}`$: the round selected on
validation; *Val-selected*: the harness of that round; *Last*: the
final-round harness. Bold: higher mean than the baseline of its block.
Underline: w/ self-evolve better than w/o.

Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
reports one evolution run per entry,
Table [7](#A6.T7 "Table 7 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
the mean of three runs per method, and
Table [8](#A6.T8 "Table 8 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
every run. Every run starts from $`h_{0}`$; at $`r^{\star}=0`$ an entry
re-evaluates $`h_{0}`$.

The verification tools help every baseline. On SWE-rebench, adding them
improves all four baselines at the selected round, and every equipped
baseline reaches or exceeds the strongest unequipped one. On
Terminal-Bench, the equipped baselines match or improve on their
unequipped versions, with margins close to the evaluation noise.

VERSE is the strongest configuration on both benchmarks. Its three-run
mean is the highest in both views on both benchmarks, and at the
selected round each of its runs scores above the mean of every baseline.
The three-run study uses the HarnessX host; the AHE-hosted run of
Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
scores higher on SWE-rebench ($`34.88`$) but is a single run.
Terminal-Bench is less sensitive to the harness in this setting: most
methods stay within about two points of blank, in line with [Wang et al.
(2026b)](#bib.bib42).

The final round is less stable without verification tools. On
SWE-rebench, three of the four baselines lose accuracy from the selected
to the final round, and a single run can lose several points. With
verification tools, the two views usually stay within two points of each
other, although some runs lose more.

## Appendix G Generalization study

This appendix asks what an evolved harness carries beyond the setting in
which it was evolved. We study three shifts, and they separate what
transfers from what does not. With the same executor and the same kind
of task, the gains of VERSE carry over from Python to newer tasks in
four other programming languages
(Section [G.1](#A7.SS1 "G.1 Transfer to new programming languages ‣ Appendix G Generalization study ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")).
When the task domain changes, the executor harness stays specific to its
source domain, and what carries over is the optimizer harness learned by
self-evolution
(Section [G.2](#A7.SS2 "G.2 Transfer to a new task domain ‣ Appendix G Generalization study ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")).
When the executor model changes, executor harnesses tuned to one model
mostly stop helping
(Section [G.3](#A7.SS3 "G.3 Transfer to a new executor model ‣ Appendix G Generalization study ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")).
Section [G.1](#A7.SS1 "G.1 Transfer to new programming languages ‣ Appendix G Generalization study ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
uses the harnesses of
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4.2 Main results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses");
the other two sections start from the harnesses of
Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
evolved with the small and mid-size models. Throughout, every score is
compared to blank, the initial harness $`h_{0}`$, measured on the same
test set with the same executor.

### G.1 Transfer to new programming languages

Figure 7: OOD accuracy by programming language. The validation-selected
harnesses of
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4.2 Main results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
on the 107 OOD tasks, split by language (mean over three test
evaluations). Each host has one color: the hollow marker is the
baseline, the filled marker is the baseline with VERSE and
self-evolution, and the line joins the two. All harnesses were evolved
on Python tasks.

All training and validation tasks of
Section [2.2](#S2.SS2 "2.2 Training, validation, and test splits ‣ 2 Setting and Motivating Observations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
are in Python, while 87 of the 107 OOD test tasks of
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4.2 Main results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
are in Go, Java, Rust, or TypeScript. Each OOD task runs in its own
released container image, which provides the toolchain of its language,
and is graded by its own test script with the SWE-rebench log parser for
that language.
Figure [7](#A7.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ G.1 Transfer to new programming languages ‣ Appendix G Generalization study ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
splits the OOD accuracy of
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 4.2 Main results ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
by language. Outside Python, the four baselines range from 1.1 points
below blank to 4.2 points above it, while VERSE with self-evolution is
3.4 to 12.6 points above blank. VERSE raises its host in 16 of the 20
pairs of host and language, and in all five languages on the
Meta-Harness and HarnessX hosts. Averaged over the four hosts, it raises
accuracy in every language: by 10.3 points in Go, 10.3 in Java, 2.3 in
Rust, 4.9 in TypeScript, and 4.2 in Python. Self-evolution matters most
on these two hosts: without it, the evolved harnesses stay near their
baselines outside Python (28.7% and 26.8%, against 29.5% and 26.1%);
with it, they reach 35.6% and 37.9%. On the AHE and Self-Harness hosts,
the tools alone raise accuracy outside Python (to 35.2% and 30.3%, from
24.1% and 29.1%).

Table 9: Cross-domain transfer (SWE $`\leftrightarrow`$ Terminal-Bench).
Each harness of
Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
is moved to the other domain, evaluated frozen (zero-shot), and evolved
for two more rounds there. An evolved optimizer inherits its harness
from the final round of the source run; a cold start begins at
$`h_{0}`$, so its zero-shot entry evaluates $`h_{0}`$ and is not bolded.
The VERSE rows start from the HarnessX-hosted VERSE harness. Avg@3
accuracy (%, $`\pm`$SEM over three test evaluations). Bold: higher mean
than the reference, which is the baseline above for “$`+`$ VERSE” rows
and the best baseline in the column for self-evolving rows.

Unlike a change of language, a change of domain changes the task itself:
a Terminal-Bench task is graded by a program that checks the final state
of its container, not by hidden tests applied to a repository diff.
Zero-shot, a transplanted executor harness can fall below the
target-domain blank: it encodes its source domain, from tool conventions
to the failures it fixes. One or two rounds of adaptation bring it back
to about the blank level. Gains beyond blank come from the inherited
optimizer harness. From SWE-rebench to Terminal-Bench, VERSE with its
inherited optimizer is above every frozen-optimizer entry in all three
columns, and with the executor harness restarted from blank, one
self-evolving configuration matches the best Terminal-Bench score of
Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
within two rounds. The reverse direction moves less, consistent with how
flat Terminal-Bench is. What crosses domains appears to be the
optimizer’s methodology, its skills, notes, and tools for diagnosing and
verifying failures, while the executor harness stays specific to its
source domain.

### G.3 Transfer to a new executor model

We hand the evolved harnesses to two open-weight executors:
Qwen3-Coder-480B and DeepSeek V3.2. Each harness from
Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
(evolved with the small model) is re-evaluated frozen (zero-shot) and
then adapted for two rounds with the new executor under the main
protocol.

Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses"),
evolved with the small model, are handed to Qwen3-Coder-480B and
DeepSeek V3.2. Each is evaluated frozen with the new executor
(zero-shot) and then evolved for two more rounds with it (Rounds 1 and
2). Evolved optimizers inherit their harness from the final round of the
source run; a cold start restarts the executor harness at $`h_{0}`$, so
its zero-shot entry evaluates $`h_{0}`$ and is not bolded. The VERSE
rows start from the HarnessX-hosted VERSE harness. Avg@3 accuracy (%,
$`\pm`$SEM over three test evaluations). Bold: higher mean than the

Zero-shot transfer to a new executor model. The zero-shot columns of
Table [10](#A7.T10 "Table 10 ‣ G.3 Transfer to a new executor model ‣ Appendix G Generalization study ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
freeze the ten SWE harnesses of
Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
and swap the executor. A harness evolved for one executor mostly stops
helping when the executor changes: averaged over the ten harnesses,
zero-shot transfer is close to zero. Two patterns remain. The equipped
version keeps its advantage over its baseline in three of four pairs on
each executor, and transfer is larger for the executor that handles
basic tool use worse under blank (Qwen). The clear exception is the
HarnessX family on Qwen: both variants sit above blank, and HarnessX
$`+`$ VERSE gives the largest zero-shot gain in the table.

To see why the HarnessX $`+`$ VERSE harness transfers, we compared all
108 Qwen test trajectories under the blank harness with the same tasks
under this harness. That harness is a minimal prompt plus an 18KB hook
file with three components: a str_replace_editor tool whose edit content
travels as a tool call argument rather than through the shell, reminders
that escalate at fixed turn counts if the agent has not yet edited a
source file or run the tests, and fixes for two common environment
errors.

Under blank, Qwen never uses an editor tool and writes every patch
through shell heredocs; in all 8 tasks that the transplant flips, the
blank trajectory dies on heredoc quoting before the patch ever lands.
Under the transplant, all 108 trajectories use the editor, all 8 flipped
tasks land their edits, and trajectories become shorter. VERSE’s own
evolved harness, which encodes behavioral fixes specific to the small
model, does not transfer. The contrast suggests a mechanism: *harness
components that fix tool use errors common across models, such as shell
quoting, transfer; components tuned to one model’s behavioral quirks do
not*.

The same transfer on Terminal-Bench.
Table [11](#A7.T11 "Table 11 ‣ G.3 Transfer to a new executor model ‣ Appendix G Generalization study ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
repeats the executor swap for the Terminal-Bench harnesses of
Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses").
Zero-shot, most transplanted harnesses stay near the blank, and
adaptation recovers the blank level but rarely exceeds it. There is no
Terminal-Bench analogue of the HarnessX jump: the Terminal-Bench
executor issues one shell command per turn and never passes edits
through shell quoting, so the friction that the transferred editor tool
removes on SWE-rebench does not arise. The one consistent signal again
favors the inherited optimizer harness: on Qwen, VERSE is the only
method above the best baseline in all three columns, and its
cold-started row also exceeds the best baseline in both adaptation
rounds.

Warm-started re-evolution on a new executor. The Round 1 and Round 2
columns of
Table [10](#A7.T10 "Table 10 ‣ G.3 Transfer to a new executor model ‣ Appendix G Generalization study ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
ask whether one evolution run can be reused across executor models. In
our runs, the best warm-started configuration outscores the best
cold-started one on each executor after two rounds. Adaptation helps
most when the starting harness is weak on the new executor and little
when it is already strong, as for HarnessX $`+`$ VERSE on Qwen. This
suggests that evolving once and then adapting for a round or two can be
a practical way to move to a new executor.

This appendix uses the small and mid-size models of

collects three ablations. The first repeats the component ablation of
Section [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Ablations ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
with these models, again on AHE without self-evolution, the host that
gains most from the tools in
Table [6](#A6.T6 "Table 6 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses").
The second keeps this configuration and its probe budget, but the
optimizer no longer chooses the probes. The third uses the setting of
Observation 1, the self-evolving optimizer with simple verification on
Meta-Harness, and restricts what the optimizer may write for itself.
Figure [8](#A8.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ Appendix H Additional ablations ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
varies the probe budget. Each configuration in this appendix is a single
evolution run, and
Table [8](#A6.T8 "Table 8 ‣ Appendix F Additional main results ‣ VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses")
shows that runs of one method can differ by several points, so we read
these ablations for their trends.

Table 12: Ablations with the small and mid-size models (SWE-rebench;
avg@3 accuracy, %, $`\pm`$SEM over three test evaluations; one evolution
run per row). Top and middle: AHE $`+`$ VERSE without self-evolution,
with components removed (top) or with the probes scheduled by a rule
instead of the optimizer (middle); both change the full configuration in
the first row. Bottom: the self-evolving optimizer with simple
verification on Meta-Harness, as in Observation 1, restricted in what it

*Val-selected*: the harness of that round; *Last*: the final-round
````
